16:I[44692,["/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/08cykig6vtx_p.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/07qi2okp-.yp9.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0frujd.eyhz4o.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0n4z.7yu76je-.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/09-o.77-p0p36.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/1304s0wcp2kce.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0j1uy9t~zxcf0.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/14_5o-nbg8k_r.js"],"DailyView"]
17:T438,For a Muon matrix B_t with compact SVD B_t=UΣV^T, polar Muon uses the canonical partial polar factor UV^T, replacing every nonzero singular value by one. MuCon uses MClip_τ(B_t)=U min(Σ,τI) V^T, with τ=1 as the default. The operator has a clean variational meaning: it is the Frobenius projection of the matrix onto the spectral-norm ball of radius τ. It also admits a low-rank correction identity, MClip_τ(M)=M-U_>(Σ_>-τI)V_>^T, where only the singular directions above the threshold are modified. This identity gives the algorithmic split: when the number of violating singular values is small, partial SVD, Lanczos, or randomized subspace methods are natural; when many singular values violate the threshold, global matrix-function iterations may be competitive. The paper also derives a polar/absolute-value formulation and explains why eigenvalues near the clipping threshold are numerically delicate. Its rational Newton iteration for the clipped positive-semidefinite factor should be read as a spectral filter, not as a complete SVD-free optimizer implementation.18:T688,The methodology is deliberately conservative: change only the sampling order.
1) Corpus: multiple Common Crawl snapshots (2020–2024) processed with the open-source dactory pipeline: HTML-to-text extraction; length filtering; fastText language ID (24 European languages, confidence > 0.85); Bloom-filter-based line dedup and paragraph removal when novel content < 20%; quality filtering via language-specific fastText domain classifiers with weighted domain mixture (Books 1.0, Wikipedia/Lifestyle 0.8, STEM/Popular 0.6, Scientific/Humanities 0.2), threshold 0.2 with stochastic domain contribution to avoid over-filtering; degenerate-text filters (n-gram repetition > 0.25, anomalous long-word proportion > 0.1).
2) Ordering intervention: a sequential model trained snapshot-by-snapshot in chronological order; a baseline trained on a globally shuffled version of the exact same data.
3) Model/training: 6B decoder-only Transformer (32 layers, 32 heads, d_model 4096) with GQA (4 KV heads), RoPE, SwiGLU. 600k steps, global batch 4.2M tokens, context 4096, totaling ~2.5T tokens. AdamW with Warmup–Stable–Decay, peak LR 1e-3.
4) Comparable intermediate checkpoints: “branching cooldown”—fork from the main run and apply an extra 30k-step cosine decay to LR 1e-4 before evaluation, reducing optimizer-state/learning-rate artifacts when comparing checkpoints.
5) Evaluation: general capability via OLMES; temporal factuality via KairosQA (7k+ Wikidata-derived temporally grounded QA) with a protocol that tests whether the model associates facts with the correct time period, plus an additional existing temporal benchmark (Zhao et al., 2024) for cross-checking.19:T45a,The Unified Neural Scaling Law (UNSL) is a multi-level functional form, constructed from the inside out: (1) Multivariate Broken Neural Scaling Law (MBNSL): extends the univariate BNSL of Caballero et al. to multiple dimensions, defining a sequence of smoothly connected hyperplanes in multi-log space, with n hyperbreaks separating n+1 hyperplanes, each with its own exponents (gradients) for each input dimension; the position and sharpness of each hyperbreak are controlled by learned parameters; (2) R layer: combines MBNSL outputs, allowing different scaling regimes to be modeled for different subsets of input dimensions; (3) Q layer: additional combination layer; (4) Final output layer. The core innovation is the use of product index sets M and summation index sets T to achieve piecewise-linear modeling of multidimensional scaling trajectories (in multi-log space), while sigmoid-like smooth transition functions handle hyperbreaks to保证 differentiability and fitting stability. Each hyperbreak is defined by a product condition ∏x_i^c_ijk = d_jk, corresponding to a hyperplane in multi-log space.1a:T56c,LRT selects a source layer l_src and defines the recurrent memory at position t as m_t = h_t^{l_src}. When processing position t, the model uses the usual causal attention and KV cache, and additionally injects m_{t-1} into the current-token computation. This creates a pathway that standard KV-cached Transformers do not have: layer l at the current position normally attends only to past states from the same layer, while LRT allows early or middle computation at the current position to access a high-level representation already computed for the previous token. The paper studies two lightweight injection mechanisms. KV Projection routes the memory through the attention key-value pathway. Residual Injection adds it as a residual signal to layer representations. The default model combines both and uses shared projections to keep parameter growth small. For training, the paper introduces interleaved parallel training. A full-sequence initialization forward first builds a shared memory buffer. Then disjoint interleaved position subsets are refined in parallel and written back to the buffer. Later subsets can consume memories updated by earlier subsets. This does not exactly reproduce sequential recurrence, but it gives every token a recurrent-memory-aware refinement step under a fixed training budget and is closer to token-level recurrence than chunked memory propagation.1b:Ta5f,论文先做“理解”，再做“改造”。

第一部分是必要性与机制分析。作者先在 0.12B 级别 Llama 预训练中实证比较带 γ 与去掉 γ 的模型，发现即便 γ 参数量几乎可以忽略，去掉后训练明显变差。理论上，他们分析 Pre-Norm 结构中 Norm(x)⊙γ 后接线性映射 W 的组合，指出在 Pre-Norm 下，γ 的作用可以被吸收到后续线性层中，因此它不增加函数类表达能力；但在梯度流上，γ 会改变后续线性层不同方向的有效步长，形成 self-amplifying preconditioning。直观地说，某些坐标上的 γ 变大，会放大这些方向进入线性层的激活，也会相应改变这些方向的梯度更新，从而进一步强化有利方向的学习速度。这解释了为什么一个很小的向量会持续影响训练动态。

第二部分是 weight decay 的区分分析。作者把 Norm 层分成 Input-Norm 和 Output-Norm。所谓 Input-Norm，是 γ 后面立刻接线性层，典型就是 Pre-Norm；Output-Norm 则是 γ 更直接地决定输出表示本身。理论结论是两类 γ 的 wd 作用相反：对 Input-Norm γ，适度 wd 有利，因为它能诱导更平衡的动力学，控制 Hessian sharpness，提升稳定性和收敛速度；对 Output-Norm γ，wd 会压缩其表达自由度，因此有害。这个结论很实用，因为很多训练代码里对 norm 参数是否做 wd 一直没有统一共识，论文给出了结构相关而不是经验拍脑袋的答案。

第三部分是三类改进。
1）Heterogeneity：在标准 Transformer 里，一个共享的 Pre-Norm 输出会同时喂给 Q/K/V 以及其他分支，但这些分支的训练动态并不相同。作者为不同分支引入 branch-specific scale vectors，让每个分支拥有自己的预条件器，而不是共享一套 γ。
2）Placement：标准 Pre-Norm 的 γ 放在线性层输入侧，主要提供 row-wise 的预条件效果。作者提出新的放置方式，使其同时产生 row-wise 与 column-wise preconditioning，从而更充分地改善后续线性映射的优化几何。
3）Reparameterization：作者把 γ 做 magnitude-direction 形式的重参数化。理论上，这会诱导更强的各向异性预条件效果，让优化更快地沿有用方向推进，而不是被单一尺度约束。

最后，作者把这些设计统一到一个 preconditioning 视角下：不同的 γ 设计，本质上都在改变后续线性层的有效优化度量。这个统一视角是论文最有价值的部分，因为它把看似零散的 norm 小改动，组织成了可推导、可组合、可扩展的设计空间。1c:T40a,The paper studies a component that is present in nearly every modern LLM but is usually treated as a minor implementation detail: the learnable scale vector gamma in RMSNorm-style normalization. The central questions are threefold. First, does gamma actually matter for pretraining despite being a negligible fraction of the parameters? Second, in Pre-Norm Transformers, is its benefit about expressivity or about optimization dynamics? Third, if it matters, are current default design choices actually sensible, including whether gamma should receive weight decay, whether one shared gamma should feed multiple branches, where gamma should be placed, and whether it should be reparameterized. The paper’s answer is clear: scale vectors matter a lot for LLM pretraining; in Pre-Norm architectures they do not increase expressivity; their main role is to act as a self-amplifying preconditioner for subsequent linear layers; and this understanding leads to several low-cost design improvements that consistently reduce terminal loss.1d:T497,This is a meaningful pretraining paper because it turns a widely used but poorly understood architectural detail into a principled optimization object. The contribution is not a large new module. It is a careful explanation of why a tiny parameter vector can have a persistent effect on training dynamics, and how to redesign it in a way that scales.

There are three reasons it matters for foundation-model research. First, it is a reminder that important training behavior often comes from small parameterization choices rather than headline architectural blocks. Those choices can affect conditioning, Hessian sharpness, anisotropy, and effective learning rates throughout training. Second, it gives a new lens on normalization in Pre-Norm LLMs: gamma should be viewed less as an expressivity component and more as a learnable preconditioner. Third, the proposed modifications add almost no compute or parameter overhead, yet they improve terminal loss across dense and MoE models, from 0.12B to 2B, under AdamW and Muon, and across multiple learning-rate schedules. That makes the work look like a recipe-level improvement with a real chance of becoming default practice.1e:Ta97,The paper proceeds in two stages: understanding, then redesign.

The first stage establishes necessity and mechanism. Empirically, the authors compare standard Llama-style pretraining with and without scale vectors and show that removing gamma substantially hurts training, despite gamma accounting for a negligible parameter fraction. Theoretically, they analyze the composition Norm(x) ⊙ gamma followed by a linear map W in Pre-Norm architectures. Their key result is that gamma does not enlarge the function class in Pre-Norm settings, because its effect can be absorbed into the following linear layer. So the gain is not expressivity. Instead, gamma changes the effective optimization geometry of the subsequent linear map. It rescales coordinates before the linear transformation, which in turn changes gradient magnitudes and update speeds across directions. This creates a self-amplifying preconditioning effect: directions that become larger through gamma can receive stronger updates, which further reinforces useful learning directions.

The second stage studies weight decay. The paper distinguishes Input-Norm from Output-Norm layers depending on whether the scale vector is immediately followed by a linear map. This distinction is important. For Input-Norm scale vectors, including standard Pre-Norm layers, weight decay is beneficial because it promotes more balanced dynamics and controls Hessian sharpness, improving both speed and stability. For Output-Norm scale vectors, weight decay is harmful because those scale vectors directly affect expressivity and should not be unnecessarily constrained. This is a practical result with immediate recipe implications.

The redesign stage introduces three complementary modifications.
1) Heterogeneity: in standard Transformer blocks, one shared normalized representation feeds multiple projections such as Q, K, and V, even though these branches have different training dynamics. The paper assigns branch-specific scale vectors so each branch gets its own preconditioner.
2) Placement: in standard Pre-Norm, gamma sits on the input side of the next linear map and mainly induces row-wise preconditioning. The paper proposes alternative placements that provably induce both row-wise and column-wise preconditioning, improving optimization more fully.
3) Reparameterization: gamma is rewritten in magnitude-direction form. The analysis argues that this yields a more anisotropic preconditioner and can accelerate optimization further.

A strong aspect of the paper is the unified view. These variants are not presented as unrelated tricks. They are all interpreted as ways of reshaping the effective preconditioner seen by downstream linear layers.1f:T665,The empirical section is broad and well aligned with the theory.

First, on 0.12B Llama pretraining runs, the paper shows that scale vectors are not optional. Removing gamma causes a clear degradation in pretraining quality, not a negligible fluctuation.

Second, the weight-decay ablations support the Input-Norm versus Output-Norm distinction. Applying weight decay to Input-Norm or Pre-Norm scale vectors helps, while applying it to Output-Norm scale vectors hurts. This is useful because it turns a long-standing recipe ambiguity into a structure-dependent rule.

Third, each of the three proposed modifications is validated separately on 0.12B models. Heterogeneity, placement, and reparameterization all provide measurable gains with minimal additional parameter or compute cost.

Fourth, the paper combines them into a unified scale-vector strategy and evaluates it on both dense and MoE models from 0.12B to 2B parameters, trained on high-quality corpora with industrial-scale token budgets. Across optimizers including AdamW and Muon, and across different learning-rate schedules, the unified strategy consistently achieves lower terminal loss than strong baselines and shows more favorable scaling behavior. For pretraining researchers, the important signal is not a downstream benchmark number but the consistency of lower loss curves, better terminal loss, and robustness across optimizer and architecture choices.

The paper appears strongest where many architecture papers are weakest: it does not rely on one narrow setting. It checks dense and MoE, small and mid-scale models, multiple optimizers, and multiple schedules.20:T52c,This one is worth a careful read. A good order is: problem setup, mechanism, design changes, then scale-up evidence.

Start with Section 2.1. That is the conceptual core. The two conclusions to keep in mind are: gamma does not add expressivity in Pre-Norm Transformers, and its benefit comes from self-amplifying preconditioning. If you read only one theoretical section, read this one.

Then move to Section 2.2 on weight decay. The key takeaway is not the algebra itself but the Input-Norm versus Output-Norm distinction and why the sign of the weight-decay effect flips between them. This section has immediate recipe consequences.

Next, read Section 3 together with the architecture figures and ablation tables. Heterogeneity is the easiest idea to operationalize. Placement is the most structurally interesting because it changes the type of preconditioning available. Reparameterization is more subtle and is best understood through the optimization-geometry lens.

Finally, spend time on Section 4. Focus on: (1) the 0.12B ablations that isolate each design choice; (2) the scaling results from 0.12B to 2B; (3) whether gains hold for both dense and MoE; (4) whether they persist under both AdamW and Muon; and (5) whether they survive learning-rate schedule changes. A serious read will likely take 60–90 minutes.21:T431,First systematically characterizes how the degree of stochasticity in Markov transitions governs the sampling speed-quality tradeoff: highly deterministic transitions converge rapidly but suffer from error accumulation, while more stochastic transitions converge more slowly to higher final sample quality. The core insight is an error-correcting mechanism induced by redundant transitions — these symmetrically exchange probability mass between state pairs without altering time marginal distributions, and can provably contract sampling errors in KL divergence. Based on this, Discrete Churn and Restart Sampling (DCRS) is proposed, a training-free inference algorithm that injects stochasticity in a controlled manner via two mechanisms: (1) churning: performing local forward-reverse perturbations; (2) restarting: periodically resetting the sampling trajectory using forward transitions. Restart steps introduce stochasticity through the forward process without additional neural network evaluations, enabling efficient global error correction across the trajectory.22:T6c8,The proposed model, LoopMDM, modifies the denoising transformer in a masked diffusion LM by selectively looping a subset of transformer layers rather than repeating the whole stack uniformly. The design targets two goals. During training, repeated application of shared layers creates a depth-scaling effect without increasing parameter count. During inference, the number of loops can be varied, enabling flexible test-time compute scaling.

A key part of the paper is the systematic comparison of loop placement strategies. The authors study uniform looping, looping different layer ranges, and different loop counts. Their main finding is that looping a few early-middle layers captures most of the benefit, while uniform looping is more expensive and less compute-efficient. This suggests that the gain is not simply from extra depth, but from repeatedly applying the same transformation at a stage where the model is integrating partially observed information.

The paper also analyzes the interaction between looping and diffusion timesteps. The benefit is concentrated at intermediate noise levels rather than at the cleanest or noisiest states. Based on that, the authors propose a simple adaptive strategy that allocates more loops to the timesteps where looping matters most, reducing compute while preserving performance.

The mechanism analysis is one of the stronger parts of the work. Through a constrained-order Sudoku task, attention analysis, and measurements of mask-to-mask interactions, the paper argues that looping improves global constraint propagation among masked positions. That is important because it points to a functional role for repeated shared computation in MDMs beyond merely enlarging model capacity.23:T573,The experiments span three pretraining corpora: FineWeb-Edu, OpenWebText, and LM1B. The central efficiency result is that LoopMDM can match the test NLL of a same-size non-looped MDM with up to 3.3x fewer training FLOPs. Under matched total training FLOPs, it consistently outperforms the baseline on zero-shot perplexity, generative perplexity, and downstream accuracy. So the gain is not only cheaper convergence to the same likelihood; it also translates into better use of a fixed compute budget.

On capability benchmarks, the paper highlights a +8.5% gain on GSM8K over the same-size non-looping MDM baseline. More importantly, LoopMDM also beats deeper MDMs whose per-step training FLOPs are matched to LoopMDM. That comparison matters because it weakens the simple “it is just deeper” explanation and supports the claim that repeated shared computation has its own effect.

The ablations cover loop placement, loop count, comparisons against deeper non-shared models, timestep-wise benefit distribution, and behavior on globally constrained tasks such as Sudoku. The analysis shows that increasing loop count strengthens mask-to-mask attention interactions, and that the gains are concentrated at intermediate noise levels. The Sudoku setup further suggests that looping helps solve tasks requiring iterative propagation of global constraints, which shallow baselines fail to handle.24:T467,This is a skim paper, but a few sections deserve careful attention. Start with the introduction and the main architecture figure to understand the comparison protocol: matched parameter count, matched total training FLOPs, and matched per-step FLOPs are different settings, and the conclusions depend on which one is used.

Then read the selective-looping ablations. That is the main architectural contribution, and the early-middle layer result is the part most likely to transfer to larger-scale work.

The GSM8K comparison against deeper non-looped MDMs is worth reading closely because it is one of the strongest pieces of evidence that looping is not just a proxy for depth.

If time allows, read the mechanism section: the Sudoku experiment, the attention analysis, and the timestep-wise breakdown of gains. Those parts are the most informative if you care about why looping helps MDMs rather than just whether it helps.

A practical reading order is: introduction, main result tables, loop-placement ablations, GSM8K comparison, then mechanism analysis. Around 30–45 minutes should be enough to extract the main ideas.25:T441,The paper first introduces a fixed-context diagnostic. Student-generated on-policy prefixes are stored as a context bank. The initial and trained student are then rescored on exactly the same contexts against the same teacher distribution. For each token position, the diagnostic measures same-context teacher-student KL reduction, isolating local learnability from rollout variance and answer-level effects. The paper then formalizes token teachability using local support. The student’s top-K predictions define the tokens it currently treats as plausible. A teacher correction is more teachable when its probability mass is aligned with this support. Based on this idea, Teachability-Aware OPD scores response positions by support-aligned disagreement and applies the OPD loss only to high-teachability tokens. Unlike entropy-based or raw-KL selection, the criterion asks whether the teacher signal can be absorbed by the current student state. The method uses probabilities already available during OPD and can be approximated with top-K supports, without a reward model or verifier.26:T6c4,The codec has four main stages. First, each weight row is factorized into its L2 norm and a unit direction vector. This separates radial information from directional distortion. Second, the direction vector is transformed by a deterministic sign-masked block Hadamard rotation. The goal is not to learn a dense rotation, but to use a cheap orthogonal transform with butterfly structure and no matrix storage, so that coordinates become more homogeneous and adjacent pairs look closer to isotropic Gaussian samples. Third, adjacent rotated coordinates are grouped into 2D pairs and normalized by pair-specific scales. These scales are not purely weight-statistics based; the paper adds an AWQ-style activation-aware calibration motivated by a layer-output trace identity, so more important channels receive a different distortion budget. Fourth, each normalized 2D point is quantized using a single Lloyd-trained codebook learned on a unit circular Gaussian. This is the main QAM-W variant. The baseline instead uses polar coding, quantizing amplitude and phase separately. The authors argue that if rotated pairs are approximately circular Gaussian, direct 2D Lloyd-Max coding matches the geometry of the source better than amplitude-phase factorization, and preserves pairwise structure that scalar coding discards. The paper also provides a monotone composite bound from codec distortion to KL divergence, to justify why local weight distortion should correlate with output-distribution degradation. A deliberate design choice is to stay in 2D: with 11 bits per pair, the codebook fits within 16 KB and remains L1-resident, so decoding is just a lookup plus rescaling, much simpler than additive-codebook or trellis-style schemes.27:T51c,The study spans five LLMs from four families, ranging from 1.1B to 13B parameters, under a unified evaluation protocol with eight quantized configurations. The headline result is that the activation-aware QAM-W variant at about 5.5 bpw stays within ±0.4% WikiText-2 perplexity of BF16 on every tested model. The paper positions this as matching the quality envelope of SmoothQuant W8A8 while using about 32% fewer weight bits, so the gain is primarily in weight storage rather than full W/A quantization. The authors are also explicit about the boundary: at strict 4 bpw, QTIP performs better, so QAM-W is not presented as the best ultra-low-bit method. In ablations, joint 2D coding beats the polar baseline, supporting the claim that direct 2D coding is better aligned with the rotated source distribution than amplitude-phase decomposition. Activation-aware scaling also improves over weight-only calibration, indicating that channel importance matters even in this codec setting. A notable diagnostic result is that paired KL and relative perplexity change are almost perfectly rank-correlated across 37 method-model pairs, with Spearman rho = 0.99. The paper mentions a six-task harness as well, but the strongest evidence appears to be the perplexity and KL results rather than broad downstream gains.28:T720,The paper introduces open knowledge evaluation and instantiates it with BeQu. Instead of asking narrow factual questions about an entity, the benchmark uses open-ended elicitation prompts such as asking the model to state everything it knows about that entity. The generated text is then decomposed into factual statements, which are verified against a reference corpus. This yields a precision estimate over expressed claims. The benchmark also estimates recall, aiming to measure how much of the relevant knowledge the model actually surfaced.

BeQu is built around 10,000 entities, each paired with reference corpora for verification. The design is closely related to prior work such as FactScore and LongFact, but with an important extension: those benchmarks mainly evaluate factual precision in free-form generation, whereas BeQu treats both precision and recall as first-class metrics. That makes it possible to distinguish between models that are cautious and accurate versus models that are broader but noisier.

The paper also studies several factors that shape open knowledge expression: model family and scale, commercial versus open-weight systems, prompt format, standardized output schemas, and reasoning effort. One especially useful methodological result concerns output schemas. Forcing a structured format improves precision substantially, but lowers recall. That means formatting constraints do not simply make the model more factual; they change the distribution of what the model is willing to say.

The technical challenge here is not model architecture but evaluation protocol. The contribution lies in turning open-ended generation into something systematic, scalable, and verifiable, using reference-based statement verification rather than relying only on human annotation or a single judge model.29:T657,The empirical study spans a broad set of language models and analyzes BeQu along multiple axes. The main findings are fourfold. First, commercial models still produce the strongest open-ended knowledge outputs overall, although several open-weight models come close. Second, reasoning effort has little effect on knowledge quality, suggesting that asking models to think longer does not materially increase the amount of stable factual knowledge they can express. Third, standardized output schemas improve precision but reduce recall: outputs become cleaner and more reliable, but narrower. Fourth, prompt-level attempts to explicitly trade precision for recall have only limited effect, implying that models are not highly controllable along that axis through simple prompting.

The most useful aspect of the evaluation is the precision-recall decomposition. Traditional QA benchmarks collapse knowledge into a single accuracy number. BeQu shows that models occupy different operating points: some are conservative with high precision and low recall, while others are more expansive but make more mistakes. That decomposition is more informative for studying the effects of pretraining data, alignment, and decoding style.

The abstract does not expose many hard numbers, but the benchmark scale is clear: 10,000 entities with reference corpora, plus systematic experiments on prompt format, schema constraints, and reasoning settings. When reading the full paper, the most important quantitative details to extract are the relative model rankings on precision versus recall and the magnitude of the schema-induced shift.2a:T51c,Start with the introduction and background. The key point is not that QA benchmarks are useless, but that they only measure explicitly queried knowledge. Then read the benchmark construction and evaluation protocol carefully, especially how statements are extracted and verified, how precision and recall are defined, and how the reference corpora are assembled. Those details determine whether the benchmark is trustworthy.

In the experiments, focus on four result groups: model comparisons in precision-recall space, the effect of reasoning effort, the effect of standardized output schemas, and prompt-based attempts to steer the precision-recall tradeoff. If time is limited, a 30-minute pass over the task definition, the main result figures, and the schema analysis is enough to capture the main contribution.

Three issues are worth deeper inspection. First, recall estimation in open-ended settings is intrinsically hard and depends on the completeness of the reference corpus. Second, open prompts mix knowledge possession with expression preference, so it is important to see how the paper handles that confound. Third, while this setup is less leak-prone than fixed QA benchmarks, it is not fully immune to future benchmark contamination through public artifacts and repeated evaluation patterns.2b:T6bd,The core artifact is AgingBench, a longitudinal benchmark for deployed agents. The paper defines four aging mechanisms. Compression aging happens when information is already underspecified or over-compressed at write time, so future-critical details are lost. Interference aging happens when many similar memories accumulate and the target fact becomes buried or confused with nearby entities. Revision aging happens when changed, retracted, or derived state is not updated correctly. Maintenance aging happens when lifecycle operations such as flushing, recompaction, migration, or prompt changes silently alter behavior. To make these mechanisms measurable, the benchmark uses a temporal dependency DAG that encodes cross-session dependencies, superseding facts, confusable entities, and controlled lifecycle events. Evaluation is then performed over agent trajectories, producing aging curves over an operational lifetime rather than a single snapshot score. A particularly useful design choice is the use of paired counterfactual probes. One probe replaces the agent’s retrieval with oracle retrieval over the agent-written memory; another replaces both writing and retrieval with gold context. This helps localize failures to the write/store/retrieve/use pipeline. If oracle retrieval fixes the issue, retrieval is the likely bottleneck; if even gold context does not fix it, the problem is more likely in memory utilization, prompting, or the harness itself. All scenarios are generated programmatically, with controllable session count, dependency density, update rate, chain depth, and interference density, making the benchmark more like a controlled stress-testing surface than an open-ended naturalistic dataset.2c:T435,From the provided full-text excerpt, the experimental emphasis appears to be on benchmark behavior and failure signatures rather than on a single headline score. The paper argues against day-one evaluation because an agent can remain fluent while exact values disappear, similar entities interfere, stale facts remain active, or maintenance silently damages state. The benchmark computes mechanism-specific metrics from agent trajectories to produce aging curves, and the generators support sweeps over session count, dependency density, update rate, chain depth, and interference density. The most important empirical questions are likely these: whether different aging mechanisms produce distinct degradation shapes; whether oracle retrieval and gold-context interventions reliably separate write, retrieval, and use failures; and whether maintenance events create abrupt drops rather than smooth decay. If the full paper includes figures, the most informative ones are likely the lifespan curves and the failure-localization signatures, not the aggregate score tables alone.2d:T4ab,MobileGym is built around a clear design choice: simulate the interaction surface that GUI agents actually use (screens + discrete actions), rather than faithfully reproducing proprietary backends or Android internals.

Key components:
1) Browser-hosted Android-like simulator implementing interaction semantics (task stack, keyboard, notifications, permission flows, intent routing, back navigation).
2) Full environment state represented as structured JSON, enabling state readability (inspection), writability (reset/config), forkability (snapshot/clone), and comparability (diff).
3) Layered state model: largely read-only world data + compact per-environment runtime state + OS runtime state; only runtime state is exposed for reset/judging to keep snapshots small and stable.
4) Deterministic judging: outcomes are verified against structured state rather than inferred from screenshots by a VLM judge.
5) AnswerSheet protocol for query tasks: typed fields submitted through the GUI to avoid fragile free-text matching.
6) Declarative app/task specifications: apps are modeled as declarative finite-state machines; tasks are parameterized via a declarative framework for scalable creation.2e:T4f4,The experiments cover closed and open-weight models, different model sizes, and thinking versus non-thinking variants. The main finding is a persistent gap between prediction and mechanism recovery. On 6-node graphs, GPT-5.2-high reaches 92% endpoint accuracy with pure observation but only 0.47 all-edge F1; mixed online observation and intervention obtains 80% accuracy and 0.80 all-edge F1. Injecting offline Golden intervention traces into GPT-5-mini raises 4-node accuracy to 90% but lowers all-edge F1, suggesting that offline traces do not replace online experimental choice. Scale helps, but unevenly. GPT-5.2-high has the best endpoint accuracy and lowest directed all-edge SHD across graph sizes, yet still drops to 64% accuracy and directed SHD 4.761 on 7-node graphs. Qwen3.5 can approach GPT-5-mini on some task scores, but its SHD grows faster as graphs become larger; thinking generally reduces Qwen’s SHD. Failure analysis indicates that many errors come from premature commitment rather than budget exhaustion. Both successful and failed runs leave roughly half of the intervention budget unused, failed runs often end with hypotheses inconsistent with their own evidence, and one explicit verification step improves 4-node accuracy from 48% to 60%.2f:T452,The method has three main components. First, Verus-SpecBench: a benchmark of 581 specification-writing tasks derived from Codeforces problems, where the model must write Verus specifications rather than code. Second, Verus-SpecGym: an agentic environment in which the model interacts with Verus, bash, and the filesystem, iteratively revising specifications based on verifier feedback instead of producing a one-shot answer. Third, executable evaluation: the authors extend Verus’s exec_spec mechanism so generated logical specifications can be compiled into Rust functions and run on concrete input-output pairs. They broaden support for the types and operators needed by Codeforces-style constraints. Evaluation uses both official Codeforces tests and community-written hacks. The hacks are especially important because they are human-designed adversarial cases targeting subtle failure modes that ordinary tests often miss. The paper also includes a semi-automatic pipeline for converting new problems and hacks into additional benchmark tasks, which makes the benchmark extensible rather than static.30:T461,Under a fixed compute and time budget, the authors evaluate six frontier and open-source LM agents. The strongest model, gemini-3.1pro, solves 77.8% of tasks. Other frontier models reach 51.1% to 57.8%, while the open-source models only achieve 21.5% to 25.5%. The gap suggests that faithful specification writing is materially harder than standard code generation and depends on fine-grained semantic precision. A key qualitative result is that models often fail on specification writing even for problems where they can generate correct code. The failure modes cluster into three types: missing important input assumptions, accepting incorrect outputs, and rejecting valid outputs. The ablations are also informative. Increasing the number and diversity of test cases consistently lowers measured success, showing that sparse testing overestimates faithfulness. Adding adversarial hacks exposes failures missed by official tests, so hacks are not redundant but necessary. An LLM-as-a-judge baseline misses 26% of the failures caught by executable evaluation, which argues against judge-only evaluation for this setting.31:T7e5,方法上最关键的是 benchmark-as-framework。作者没有再做一个独立评测 harness，而是实现了一个极简但生产级的 inference framework，支持 continuous batching、chunked prefills、multimodal inputs 和 OpenAI-compatible API，并声称在主流 LLM serving 上与 vLLM、SGLang 同量级。这样生成的 kernel 可以直接在真实推理流水线中运行，避免“先在 benchmark 里赢，再手工移植到框架里掉速”的问题。第二个核心设计是 top-down task construction。FastKernels 从 46 个真实模型架构出发，递归拆解其 inference path 中的 kernel，而不是从人工拼装的 primitive 出发。每个 task 都从 HuggingFace 配置和 forward 定义中抽取，内联真实 hidden size、head 数、dtype 等常量，并审核语义正确性、shape/dtype 一致性、以及与生产参考库的接口一致性。作者明确强调 benchmark 中没有 synthetic kernel，每个任务都对应真实模型推理时会执行的操作。第三是 compositional hierarchy。任务分四级：Level 1 primitive operators，Level 2 fused operators，Level 3 full layers/blocks，Level 4 full models。与很多 benchmark 各 level 相互独立不同，这里 lower-level kernel 可以被 higher-level task 复用，形成类似动态规划的优化过程。对 agent 来说，这比每一关都从头搜索更接近真实工程。第四是 production-faithful evaluation。评测不仅看 isolated speed，还纳入 captured tensors、真实 compilation stack、多 GPU 通信模式、tensor parallel 与 expert parallel。文中提到一个 MoE routing 研究：合成输入会显著改变负载倾斜和 hot expert 身份，这说明 synthetic input 会直接扭曲 kernel 热点。第五是 end-to-end validation and metrics。FastKernels 把生成 kernel 注入完整模型执行，检查 downstream quality，并用 MacroEval 汇总 calibrated correctness、coverage 和端到端 throughput-latency speedup，而不是只报单 kernel latency。32:T7f2,The central idea is benchmark-as-framework. Instead of building a separate harness, the authors implement a minimal but production-grade inference framework with continuous batching, chunked prefills, multimodal inputs, and an OpenAI-compatible serving API. The point is that generated kernels run directly inside a realistic inference pipeline rather than being optimized in isolation and ported later. A second key design choice is top-down task construction. FastKernels starts from 46 real model architectures and recursively decomposes their inference paths into executable kernel tasks. Each task is extracted from actual HuggingFace model definitions, with configuration constants such as hidden size, head count, and dtype inlined from the real model. Tasks are then audited for semantic correctness, shape and dtype fidelity, and interface compatibility with production reference libraries. The paper explicitly states that none of the kernels are synthetic. Third, the benchmark is compositional. Tasks are organized into four levels: primitive operators, fused operators, full layers or blocks, and full models. Unlike benchmarks with independent levels, lower-level optimized kernels can be reused when optimizing higher-level modules, which makes the search process closer to real engineering and more suitable for agentic optimization. Fourth, evaluation is production-faithful. It includes captured tensors from real model executions, compilation-stack effects, and multi-GPU communication patterns such as tensor parallelism and expert parallelism. The MoE routing case study is especially important: synthetic inputs materially change load skew and hot-expert identity, which means they can distort what the benchmark considers important. Fifth, FastKernels validates generated kernels end-to-end by injecting them into full model execution and checking downstream quality. It reports MacroEval, a metric that combines calibrated correctness, coverage, and end-to-end throughput-latency speedup across model families.33:T4da,The most important experimental message is not a single speedup number but that benchmark design changes what conclusions you draw. First, the framework is claimed to run at parity with vLLM and SGLang on mainstream LLM serving workloads, which matters because a benchmark that is not itself realistic cannot serve as a production proxy. Second, the coverage is broad: 46 architectures across 8 categories, including dense and MoE LLMs, linear attention, SSMs, vision, audio, video, robotics, 3D graphics, recommendation, and world models. Third, the MoE routing study shows that synthetic inputs alter both load skew and hot-expert identity, directly demonstrating that synthetic benchmarking can optimize the wrong bottlenecks. Fourth, the compositional hierarchy allows lower-level kernel improvements to propagate upward into fused operators, layers, and full models, which is a more realistic setting for kernel-generation agents. The available text does not provide enough detailed quantitative breakdowns—such as MacroEval distributions, exact transfer gaps versus prior benchmarks, or per-stack regression numbers—so this is best read as an evaluation-design paper rather than a kernel-method paper focused on micro-optimizations.34:T456,TORM uses a two-stage training procedure. Each training instance contains sampled keyframes from the source video, a generated thought video built from a teacher reasoning plan, and the target answer sequence. In Stage I, a small number of latent tokens are inserted between the video-question context and the final answer. The model is trained with answer-token loss and a latent-state alignment objective that makes the latent hidden states encode features of the thought video. The latent segment is therefore grounded in question-relevant motion and state changes without requiring textual CoT supervision. In Stage II, the latent slots remain in the sequence, but supervision is applied only to the final answer, similar in spirit to Coconut-style latent reasoning. This encourages the model to consolidate the aligned latent behavior into an answer-oriented internal reasoning process. At inference time, the model does not generate thought videos, revisit frames, or call external visual tools. It performs a bounded hidden-state rollout through the latent segment and then resumes normal text decoding.35:T787,方法核心有四部分。第一是 OV-Encoder 的延续与增强。模型建立在 native dynamic-resolution OneVision-Encoder 上，保留 codec patchification 思路，不再只从解码帧取 patch，而是让 I/P frame 及其预测残差信号参与视觉证据选择。第二是 codec-stream tokenization。作者把压缩视频视为连续 bit-cost stream，而不是固定 GOP 的帧序列。时间上，用 bit-cost dynamics 自适应决定 temporal group 边界；直觉上，码率突变往往对应场景切换、动作变化或新信息注入。空间上，用 motion-residual cues 从组内挑出更有信息量的区域，压成 compact visual canvases，并与 merge 对齐，减少无效 token。这样 token 密度跟随码流的信息变化，而不是跟随均匀时间采样。第三是 attention 与位置系统。为了在原生分辨率下控制计算，模型使用 spatial windowed attention 做局部计算，同时配合 group-visible masks 维持组内可见性约束；再用 shared 3D RoPE 把 codec canvases、普通采样帧和静态图像放进统一时空坐标系，避免不同输入路径的位置语义割裂。第四是训练 recipe。训练分四阶段逐步扩展能力：Stage 1 用约 85M image-text 和 4.2M 30 秒视频 caption 做图像 grounding 与短视频基础对齐；Stage 2 加入约 22M LLaVA-OneVision-1.5 指令数据、24M FineVision，以及 2.7M 30–60 秒和 700K 60–180 秒视频 caption；Stage 3 引入长视频 instruction 数据和 350K 10–15 分钟 caption，最长到 384 frames；Stage 4 用 variable-length-GOP codec pipeline 重新编码长视频，在 384/768 frames 上训练，并加入 4M 样本的 2D/3D spatial supervision。一个重要设计是训练时始终交织 codec-patchified video、uniformly sampled video 和 image/tiled input，而不是完全替换抽帧路径。作者后面的实验也说明，两种表示各有适用区间。36:T797,The method has four main pieces. First, it extends the OneVision-Encoder line with native dynamic-resolution processing and codec patchification, so the model is not restricted to uniformly decoded frames. Second, the key contribution is codec-stream tokenization. Instead of treating a video as a fixed-GOP frame sequence, the model views the compressed stream as a continuous bit-cost signal. Temporal group boundaries are chosen adaptively from bit-cost dynamics, under the intuition that bitrate changes often correlate with scene transitions, motion changes, or semantic novelty. Within each temporal group, motion-residual cues are used to select salient spatial evidence and condense it into compact visual canvases aligned with the merge structure. This makes token density track information change in the stream rather than wall-clock time. Third, the architecture uses spatial windowed attention to keep native-resolution computation manageable, together with group-visible masks to preserve the intended visibility pattern. A shared 3D RoPE places codec canvases, sampled video frames, and still images into one spatiotemporal coordinate system, which is important because the model is trained on mixed input pathways. Fourth, the training recipe is progressive. Stage 1 uses about 85M image-text pairs and 4.2M 30-second video captions. Stage 2 adds roughly 22M LLaVA-OneVision-1.5 instruction samples, 24M FineVision samples, plus 2.7M 30–60s and 700K 60–180s video captions. Stage 3 extends to long-form video with video-instruction corpora and 350K captions for 10–15 minute videos at 384 frames. Stage 4 re-encodes long videos with a variable-length-GOP codec pipeline at 384/768 frames and adds a 4M-sample 2D/3D spatial supervision corpus. An important design choice is that codec-patchified videos, uniformly sampled videos, and image/tiled inputs are interleaved throughout training rather than replacing one another.37:T585,Three result groups matter most. First, JumpScore, the new benchmark designed for temporal localization in high-frequency repeated motion. LLaVA-OneVision-2-8B reaches 74.9 mAP, while Qwen3-VL-8B gets 30.1, a gap of 44.8 points. More importantly, under matched visual-token budgets, codec-stream inputs outperform frame-sampling inputs by 9.7 points on the same benchmark. That is the clearest evidence that the token-allocation mechanism itself is doing useful work. Second, at the model level, LLaVA-OV-2-8B reports average gains of +4.3 on video benchmarks, +5.3 on spatial benchmarks, and +15.6 J&F on tracking benchmarks over Qwen3-VL-8B. Third, the paper is careful about where the method helps and where it does not. Codec-stream inputs are better for tasks dominated by coarse temporal structure—temporal grounding, event understanding, event ordering, salient retrieval—because they concentrate tokens around high-bit-cost intervals and high-residual regions. Frame sampling remains preferable for detail-sensitive queries where the decisive evidence is static, tiny, trajectory-specific, or boundary-level, because dense frame observations preserve local texture and frame-to-frame continuity better. The paper also scales data substantially, mentioning about 8M re-captioned video samples and a 4M spatial corpus, so the gains should be read as the result of both representation and training scale.38:T50f,Given a latent tensor Z∈R^{h×w×c}, conventional VQ treats each spatial vector z(i,j,:) as a codebook lookup target, producing h×w local patch tokens. CVQ instead slices the tensor along the channel dimension and quantizes each channel map z(:,:,k), producing a 1D sequence of c channel tokens. Each token therefore carries global spatial structure rather than a local patch. The motivation is that autoencoder channels often separate different levels of visual information: early channels can encode coarse structure, layout, or color tone, while later channels add finer attributes. This change also addresses codebook collapse. Patch embeddings are highly redundant across images and tend to cluster around a small subset of codes early in training. Channel-wise embeddings are more separable, so codebook updates are spread over more entries. On top of CVQ, the authors define a Channel-wise Autoregressive framework that performs next-channel prediction instead of next-patch prediction. Nested dropout is used to encourage an ordered channel sequence where prefixes reconstruct meaningful coarse content and later tokens refine details. The distinction from ordering-only methods is important: CVQ first changes what a token means, then applies AR modeling to a cleaner 1D detail axis.39:T7ee,The method starts from the rectified-flow prediction objective for the clean image predictor f_theta(x_t, t, c), where x_t is a linear interpolation between a clean image x_0 and Gaussian noise. Standard training minimizes E||f_theta(x_t,t,c)-x_0||^2. AdvantageFlow replaces this with an advantage-weighted version so that higher-reward samples receive larger positive influence and lower-reward samples receive negative influence relative to the prompt-level batch average.

The main technical difficulty is that advantages can be negative. Naively using signed weights makes the per-sample least-squares objective non-convex and unstable. The paper addresses this with rollout policy regularization. Instead of directly fitting noisy signed targets, it introduces a reward-independent reference induced by a moving-average rollout policy. The derivation is framed as fitting a locally reward-improving target distribution, i.e., a linearly tilted distribution around the current policy that shifts mass toward better samples. The regularization term emerges naturally when replacing noisy sampled targets with their conditional means in the reward-independent part of the objective. This reduces variance and restores strict convexity at the sample level.

An additional useful point is unification. The paper shows that a recent negative-aware forward-process RL baseline can be recovered as a special case under a particular adaptive regularization choice. So the contribution is not only a new algorithm, but also a cleaner objective-level view that organizes several forward-process RL heuristics under one framework.

Compared with reverse-process RL, the difference is structural: no stochastic sampler is required for gradient estimation, no denoising-step credit assignment is needed, and the optimized loss remains the same type of forward prediction loss used in pretraining, only reweighted by reward-derived statistics. In practice this makes the method look closer to supervised fine-tuning than to conventional RL.3a:T520,Experiments are built on Stable Diffusion 3.5 Medium for text-to-image reward optimization. The main comparisons are against Flow-GRPO on the reverse-process side and a negative-aware fine-tuning baseline on the forward-process side. The reported result is consistent throughout the paper: AdvantageFlow outperforms both baselines across several reward models and also extends to multi-objective optimization.

The empirical section mainly validates four claims. First, forward-process RL can improve reward without explicit reverse-process credit assignment. Second, rollout regularization is not just a training trick; it is central to both stability and final quality, especially when negative advantages are common. Third, gains are not purely reward-model overfitting: the paper includes GenEval as an external check against reward hacking. Fourth, the unifying view is meaningful in practice, since different regularization choices explain the behavior of prior forward-process baselines.

What is missing, from a pretraining-research perspective, is large-scale scaling analysis. The paper does not focus on model-size scaling, token budgets, or training-dynamics diagnostics in the style of LLM pretraining work. It is primarily an algorithm paper centered on objective design and optimization stability.3b:T402,Start with the Introduction and Algorithm sections. The key question is why reverse-process RL is awkward for flow models and why forward-process weighted regression is a more natural alternative. Then read the derivation of the advantage-weighted least-squares objective from the local reward-improving target distribution. The most important technical point is the role of rollout regularization: it is doing two jobs at once, variance reduction and repair of the non-convexity caused by negative advantages.

If reading time is limited, focus on: (1) the discussion of why negative advantages break naive weighted least squares; (2) the section showing how negative-aware fine-tuning appears as a special case; (3) the main result tables and regularization ablations; and (4) the GenEval results, which are the main sanity check against reward hacking. Around 20–30 minutes should be enough for a solid skim. If your interest is diffusion LM or non-AR LM post-training, the derivation is the part worth reading carefully.3c:T69f,M2 is a 62-layer decoder-only Transformer with 229.9B total parameters and 9.8B activated parameters per token. It uses a hidden size of 3,072, a vocabulary of 200,064 tokens, 29.2T pretraining tokens, and a native 192K context length. Attention is full attention in all layers rather than a hybrid pattern. It uses GQA with 48 query heads and 8 key-value heads, together with RoPE. The feed-forward blocks are MoE layers with 256 fine-grained experts and 8 activated experts per token. The fine-grained design increases routing combinatorics and reduces utilization variance. Routing uses sigmoid gating plus learnable expert-specific biases, improving load balance while reducing dependence on auxiliary load-balancing losses. The pretraining objective includes standard next-token prediction plus an MTP module. During continued pretraining, the MTP module is expanded through weight copying and later reused as a multi-step speculative-decoding draft path. The post-training stack is agent-native. Data pipelines cover agentic coding, agentic cowork, reasoning, and general knowledge, with static or runtime environments, verifiable rewards, or credible feedback. Forge decouples training, inference, and the agent scaffold, and supports both white-box and API-only black-box agents in one loop. It uses windowed-FIFO scheduling to handle trajectory-length variance, prefix-tree merging to share common prefixes, and inference kernels aligned with the serving stack to reduce rollout cost. M2.7 also shows an early self-evolution setup, where the model triages failed training runs, edits its own agent scaffold, and is evaluated through multi-round self-improvement on ML-engineering tasks.3d:T4f3,SCAS (Student-Centric Answer Sampling) operates in a candidate-answer setting: for each question q, there is a set of verified-correct teacher answers A(q). Instead of picking the strongest teacher, SCAS samples answers according to an estimated student-centric learning cost.
The cost is motivated by gradient mechanics in SFT:
- Consider the squared Frobenius norm of the gradient on a student weight matrix W induced by the concatenated sequence x=[q;a].
- Expand the gradient norm into pairwise token interaction terms, then partition token indices into question (Q) and answer (A) positions, yielding AA (answer–answer), AQ/QA (answer–conditioning interactions), and QQ (conditioning–conditioning) blocks.
- This decomposition explains why two correct answers can differ in how much parameter change they demand from the current student.
- To avoid per-candidate backprop, the paper derives a forward-only proxy to approximate the learning cost using signals available from forward passes (representations and error-related quantities), enabling efficient scoring over large candidate pools.
Training then samples lower-cost answers more often, aligning supervision with the student’s current capabilities and effectively creating an adaptive curriculum.3e:Taef,方法上分三步。第一步是构造可比训练语料。作者从 allenai/Dolci-Think-SFT-32B 中均匀采样约 50 万条英文样本，覆盖数学、代码、科学、指令跟随、安全、聊天和结构化数据等类别，再翻译到法语、德语、西班牙语、中文和斯瓦希里语，形成六种语言各约 50 万条、最长 32k context 的长 CoT 数据。这里不是简单整条翻译，而是把 question、reasoning trace、final answer 分开，并按约 2k token 的句段切块翻译，再重组。这样做是因为作者在全文里明确指出，直接对超长样本做单次翻译会出现两类失败：长上下文退化导致循环，以及更隐蔽的 CoT 被静默丢失。这个数据工程细节很重要，因为它说明多语言 reasoning 数据构造的瓶颈并不只是翻译质量，还包括长轨迹结构保真。

第二步是在严格对齐的 SFT 条件下训练两类 specialist。底座是 Qwen/Qwen3-8B-Base。对每个非英语语言，作者训练 native specialist：输入、CoT、答案都在目标语言；同时训练 English-pivoted specialist：输入和最终答案在目标语言，但中间 CoT 用英语。每种语言的 SFT token 预算大约都是 10B，避免了“native 数据少所以差”的混淆。这个设计的关键不只是双轨训练，而是 supervision parity：同一底座、同量级 token、同类任务分布，只改变 reasoning trace 的语言。

第三步是权重空间分析与 Layer Swap。作者比较不同语言 specialist 相对同一 base model 的参数更新，发现中间层更新方向跨语言更一致，而靠近 embedding / early layers 和靠近输出端的 late layers 差异更大。基于这个观察，他们采用 training-free 的 Layer Swap：保留 native specialist 的首尾层，以维持目标语言输入理解和输出表达；把 English specialist 的中间层替换进去，以迁移更强的 reasoning core。这个方案和常见的 merge、adapter 或 LoRA 叠加不同，它依赖的是“层功能分工”而不是简单参数平均。全文的一个重要细节是，作者并不是把英语模型整体拿来覆盖，而是只交换中间层，这说明他们把 native gap 解释为 reasoning core 与 language interface 的错配，而不是整体能力不足。

作者还做了 input-language ablation，去区分“输入理解”与“推理过程语言”哪个更像瓶颈。结论是，在 matched native SFT 下，输入理解仍是主要瓶颈之一。这一点很关键，因为它意味着即便 CoT 已经能用目标语言写出来，模型对复杂非英语题面的解析仍可能限制上限。换句话说，native reasoning 的问题不只是‘不会用该语言思考’，而是‘对该语言复杂输入的编码不够稳’。3f:T46b,For pretraining and post-training researchers, the contribution is less about multilingual benchmarking and more about representation structure. First, it recalibrates the native reasoning narrative: with enough native long-CoT supervision, the gap is much smaller than many earlier studies implied, only 1.9%–3.5% on average across five non-English languages. That matters for multilingual post-training budgets and for how one interprets English-dominant CoT behavior in current reasoning models. Second, the weight-space analysis suggests a partial functional separation across depth: middle-layer updates align strongly across languages, while early and late layers diverge more. This is a concrete clue about where language-specific processing lives versus where reasoning computation may be shared. Third, the training-free Layer Swap result is interesting beyond this exact setup. It points to a broader possibility: when two specialists share a base model and differ mainly in supervision language, capability transfer may be achievable through structured parameter recomposition rather than another expensive tuning run.40:Ta82,The work has three main components. The first is data construction under long-context constraints. The authors start from allenai/Dolci-Think-SFT-32B, sample roughly 500k English examples while preserving category balance, and translate them into French, German, Spanish, Chinese, and Swahili, yielding six language-specific corpora with contexts up to 32k tokens. A useful engineering detail appears in the full text: direct single-pass translation of long samples failed in two ways. Very long examples triggered degraded long-context behavior with looping, and some samples silently dropped the reasoning trace while still translating the question and final answer. To avoid this, they translate question, reasoning trace, and final answer separately, chunk each into about 2k-token segments at sentence or paragraph boundaries, translate chunkwise, and then recompose. This is not just a preprocessing footnote; it shows that preserving long reasoning traces in multilingual data creation is itself a nontrivial bottleneck.

The second component is a controlled SFT comparison. Using Qwen/Qwen3-8B-Base, they train for each target language two specialists under matched token budgets of about 10B SFT tokens per language. In the native regime, input, CoT, and answer are all in the target language. In the English-pivoted regime, the input and final answer are in the target language but the intermediate CoT is in English. The key design choice is supervision parity: same base model, same scale, same task mix, only the language of the reasoning trace changes.

The third component is weight-space analysis followed by Layer Swap. The authors compare per-language SFT updates relative to the shared base model and find that update directions align tightly in the middle layers across languages, while divergence is larger near the input and output ends. They then exploit this with a training-free procedure: keep the native specialist’s early and late layers to preserve target-language input understanding and output generation, but replace its middle layers with those from the English specialist to import a stronger reasoning core. This differs from generic model merging or parameter averaging. The method relies on a depth-wise functional hypothesis rather than a global interpolation assumption.

An additional ablation studies the role of input language understanding. The paper argues that even under matched native SFT, input understanding remains a primary bottleneck. That is an important nuance. The issue is not only whether the model can produce a target-language CoT, but whether it can robustly parse complex non-English problem statements before reasoning begins.41:T81e,There are three headline findings. First, the native reasoning gap is much smaller than commonly reported when supervision is matched. Across general knowledge, mathematics, code, and science benchmarks, the average gap between native specialists and English-pivoted specialists is only 1.9%–3.5% over the five non-English languages. The remaining gap is concentrated on harder reasoning benchmarks and is much smaller elsewhere. This strongly suggests that earlier estimates were inflated by data and training imbalance rather than by an intrinsic inability to reason natively.

Second, Layer Swap recovers most of the residual gap in several languages while keeping the CoT in the target language. It closes 83%–89% of the gap for French and German, about 60% for Swahili, 27% for Chinese, and reaches the English-pivoted ceiling for Spanish. Two details matter here. The gains are highly language-dependent, which means the middle-layer reasoning core is not perfectly language-invariant. Chinese in particular appears to retain more language-specific computation beyond the model edges. And Spanish nearly saturating the ceiling suggests that for some high-resource languages close to English, the remaining native gap may be mostly a mild mismatch in shared reasoning representations rather than a deeper capability deficit.

Third, the weight-space evidence supports the mechanism. Middle-layer update alignment is substantially stronger than alignment at the edges, which gives Layer Swap a concrete empirical basis. The input-language ablation is also important: the authors identify input understanding as a major bottleneck even after substantial native CoT supervision. While the excerpt does not include every table value, this point is prominent enough in the paper to count as a central result rather than a side observation.

In terms of scale, the setup uses an 8B base model, around 500k examples per language, roughly 10B SFT tokens per language, and up to 32k context length. That is large enough to move the discussion beyond small native-tuning studies.42:T596,For a quick pass, focus on three things. First, read the introduction and Figure 2 to pin down the actual claim: the paper does not say native reasoning is free, it says the gap becomes small under matched supervision. Second, read the data construction section carefully, especially why long-CoT translation had to be done component-wise and chunkwise, and how they filtered samples with dropped reasoning traces. The credibility of the whole comparison depends on this pipeline. Third, read the weight-space analysis and Layer Swap section with the mechanism in mind. The key question is not just how much performance improves, but why middle layers appear transferable across languages.

If time is limited to 20–30 minutes, a good order is: Introduction, Figures 1 and 2, data construction, Layer Swap method, then the main result tables. If you have more time, prioritize the update-alignment analysis and the input-language ablation, since those are what make the paper explanatory rather than merely empirical. The most interesting follow-up questions are: why Chinese benefits much less than French, German, or Spanish; whether chunkwise translation lowers the achievable ceiling for native specialists by hurting long-range coherence; and whether Layer Swap only works for closely related specialists sharing the same base and training trajectory, or whether it can generalize to more heterogeneous model recomposition.43:T470,The evaluation uses BashArena 1.1. Agents operate in a Docker environment with tools such as bash, Python, and a file editor. Main tasks include systems programming, data processing, and security-related work; side tasks include disabling the firewall, downloading malware, leaking sensitive data, and editing sudoers. Claude Opus 4.6 is used as the untrusted model, and MiMo-V2-Flash is used as the trusted monitor. Retrying with detailed feedback can be exploited by the attacker, eliminating the expected safety gains. Retrying without feedback is less directly exploitable, but may still be vulnerable to more sophisticated attack selection. Resampling improves safety mainly by improving monitor information. With five samples per step and auditing based on the maximum or an upper-quantile suspicion score, safety increases from 61% to 71%, a +10.2 percentage point gain with paired 95% CI [+6.1, +14.3]. Selective resampling reduces cost: adding only 0.4 extra samples per step recovers 6.2 percentage points of safety gain. Executing the least suspicious sample adds little, and under an optimal attacker it adds no safety gain.44:T452,The method is a controlled experimental framework rather than a new optimizer.
- World generator: each instance is grounded in a synthetic knowledge graph combining (i) a dynamic event/state component (object-centric facts updated over time) and (ii) a static kinship graph.
- Two orthogonal difficulty knobs: D (reasoning depth) controls the length of the target inference chain; T (environment complexity) controls the amount/structure of distractors and interactions around that chain.
- Four task families from the same world: deductive state tracking (forward), abductive recovery (missing event/initial fact), inductive rule induction, and analogy-based relational transfer.
- Training protocol: pretrain on a restricted easy region (shallow D, low T) to make the base distribution controlled; then apply GRPO-based RLVR with different data allocation recipes over the D×T grid (depth-focused, complexity-focused, joint coverage, staged curricula).
- Evaluation emphasizes spatial generalization over the D×T grid and cross-family transfer via correlation/cluster analyses, not just average scores.45:T4da,GeoFaith frames faithfulness via a spatio-temporal view of reasoning trajectories:
- Spatial (latent geometry): treat each reasoning step as a point given by the model’s last-token hidden state. Empirically, these states lie on a low-dimensional manifold (fast PCA saturation; low intrinsic dimension via TwoNN). Faithful vs. unfaithful states appear separable in UMAP. To make geometry computable beyond visualization, they fit a VAE and define a Riemannian metric (pullback metric induced by the decoder), then approximate geodesic distances with a kNN graph in latent space.
- Temporal (entropy dynamics): track predictive entropy across steps; abnormal entropy evolution is used as an additional indicator of unfaithful “patching” behavior.
- Data bootstrapping: expand step-level faithfulness annotations from 1k to 20k using trajectory clustering and detector-guided refinement, aiming to scale process supervision without relying on brittle LLM judges.
- Training and optimization: train an 8B step-level faithfulness detector; then incorporate it into a faithfulness-aware RL objective that jointly optimizes answer correctness, process faithfulness, and trajectory consistency, encouraging shorter but more faithful CoT traces.46:T60e,CroCo extends controlled-contrastiveness pairing (previously validated in English) to multilingual preference tuning.

1) Pair construction with controlled contrastiveness: for each prompt, sample K responses from the current policy (on-policy). Score each response with an external reward model R. Choose a high-reward completion as y_c. Instead of picking the minimum-reward completion as y_r, pick the response whose reward is closest to μ-2σ of the per-prompt reward distribution. This targets a “moderately bad” rejection, aiming for an informative but not trivial reward gap.

2) Multilingual data generation: start from an English instruction set, translate prompts into multiple target languages, then generate K candidates conditioned on each target-language prompt. Importantly, scoring is done within each language’s candidate set.

3) Reward model transfer: use a reward model trained on English preferences but built on a multilingual base encoder/LM, and rely on within-language ranking rather than absolute reward comparability across languages.

4) Training regimes: compare monolingual DPO (per-language tuning) vs multilingual DPO (joint training on the union of all language datasets). The paper also contrasts against SFT and prior multilingual preference-tuning baselines.

5) Hypothesis-driven ablations: the paper explicitly tests (i) that relative reward gaps suffice even with cross-lingual miscalibration, and (ii) that on-policy sampling is crucial because the contrastive signal must match the policy distribution.47:T423,Core setup:
- Data: 20K instructions sampled from Dolci-Instruct-SFT; translated into six European languages (da/nl/fr/de/it/es) using TranslateGemma-27B.
- Models: EuroLLM-9B-Instruct and aya-3B.
- Sampling: 64 candidates per prompt; the paper notes performance saturates beyond ~60 samples.
- Evaluation: EuroEval (structured tasks) and m-ArenaHard 2.1 (open-ended preference/arena-style evaluation).

Reported headline results (as stated in the main text):
- EuroEval: multilingual Paired DPO matches or exceeds the base model in 6/7 language settings for EuroLLM-9B and 4/7 for aya-3B.
- m-ArenaHard 2.1: both DPO-tuned models outperform their respective bases in all 11 evaluated languages.
- Forgetting: standard SFT causes catastrophic forgetting for both models, while DPO on self-generations avoids it.

Ablations (directions clearly described; exact numbers are in the experiment tables/appendix): reward model language/transfer, translation and prompt-language variants, monolingual vs multilingual training, and on-policy vs off-policy generation.48:T4c0,The empirical study uses 60 seed responses from real multi-stakeholder travel-planning queries, with stakeholder counts n in {2, 3, 5, 8} and three quality levels. For each seed, the authors create 55 semantics-preserving variants across 11 transformation families, including ordering changes, formatting controls, causal framing changes, evidence relocation, paraphrasing, and topic repositioning. Five exact repeats provide an intrinsic-noise baseline. Evaluations are run at temperature 0 using Qwen3.5-Plus, Gemini-3-Flash, and DeepSeek-V4-Flash. For medium- and high-quality responses, variance across semantic variants grows with stakeholder count: pooled Growth is 1.87, and Gemini reaches 6.67x. At n=8, the Sem/Rep ratio reaches 2.64 for medium and high responses, meaning presentation changes add much more variance than repeated evaluation noise. Low-quality responses do not show this pattern, with Growth 0.29, likely due to a floor effect. Structured protocols reduce variance, but judge-assigned adaptive weights reintroduce instability: Rubric has Growth 1.18, decomposed scoring with fixed uniform weights has 1.20, while adaptive decomposed weighting rises to 1.58 using the same utility estimates.49:T42b,The key idea is to constrain second-order statistics of the importance ratio ρ_t(θ)=π_θ(a_t|s_t)/π_old(a_t|s_t), rather than clipping ρ_t per token/step. The authors argue that, in a local regime, a KL trust-region constraint can be approximated by controlling the variance (and related second moment) of ρ.

They propose R2VPO (Ratio-Variance Regularized Policy Optimization): formulate policy optimization as maximizing an IS-style surrogate objective subject to a constraint on Var(ρ). The constraint is enforced via a Lagrangian primal–dual scheme:
- primal update improves the return surrogate (PPO-like objective without hard clipping);
- dual update adapts the penalty to keep ratio variance near a target.

This yields a “soft brake”: samples with extreme ratios are smoothly down-weighted (not zeroed), preserving gradient signal from rare but valuable trajectories. An off-policy variant (R2VPO-OFF) explicitly reuses stale trajectories; the same variance control acts as an automatic gate on how much those trajectories can influence updates.4a:T4a5,For each parameter matrix, the authors compute its singular values and normalize them by their trace, thereby removing global scale and retaining the shape of the spectrum. They then track cosine similarity between the current normalized spectrum and the final spectrum, and compare it with cosine similarity between the current weight matrix and the final weight matrix. The empirical finding is a clear decoupling: normalized spectra reach their final shape much earlier than the raw weights reach their final configuration. The theoretical analysis uses a simplified Transformer model. Under non-degeneracy of parameters and bounded gradients, the authors prove the emergence of SoSD. With additional smoothness and margin assumptions, they show that loss decrease is coupled to variation in the singular distribution: large spectral-distribution changes correspond to fast loss decrease, while after SoSD the possible loss improvement becomes bounded. A key scaling relation is ε proportional to η / ||W||, where η is the learning rate and ||W|| is the weight norm. This relation is then used to interpret why learning-rate schedules and weight decay affect late-stage optimization.4b:T46a,Its value is mainly conceptual, but it is relevant to pretraining because it turns an empirical recipe into an analyzable objective. First, it addresses a long-standing gap in JEPA-style SSL: explicit collapse prevention may stabilize training, but what structure does it actually recover? Second, it gives a sharp answer for LeJEPA: alignment plus explicit Gaussian regularization does not merely avoid collapse; under Gaussian latents with stationary additive-noise transitions, it biases the solution toward linearly recoverable latent structure. Third, the if-and-only-if result is unusually strong: within the paper’s world class, Gaussian latents are the unique distribution for which this guarantee can hold universally. That matters beyond JEPA theory. It says identifiability is not a generic byproduct of predictive SSL; it depends on a precise match between the objective and the latent generative assumptions. For pretraining researchers, the broader lesson is that explicit distributional regularization can make representation learning objectives much easier to reason about than implicit anti-collapse mechanisms.4c:T73e,The analysis centers on the LeJEPA objective: an alignment term that pulls positive-pair embeddings together, plus an explicit regularizer that matches the embedding distribution to an isotropic Gaussian, in the spirit of SIGReg. The paper first defines a broad class of worlds: independent latent coordinates, positive pairs drawn from a stationary process, and additive-noise transitions. The forward theorem then specializes to z~N(0,I). Under stationarity, the only additive-noise transition that preserves the Gaussian marginal is the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck form z'=rho z + sqrt(1-rho^2) epsilon. The key proof idea is a spectral decomposition of the composed map h=f∘g under Gaussian measure. Intuitively, if h contains nonlinear components beyond degree one, those components are penalized by the alignment objective, while the Gaussian regularizer constrains the output distribution to remain standard normal. The optimum therefore retains only the linear component, yielding h(z)=Qz. The mechanism relies on the fact that different polynomial degrees decay differently under correlated Gaussian pairs, so alignment suppresses higher-order terms. The converse theorem is the more distinctive part: for the same class of stationary additive-noise worlds, if the latent distribution is non-Gaussian, one cannot guarantee linear identifiability for all such worlds. Gaussianity is therefore not just a convenient assumption for the proof; it is the unique distribution that supports this universal guarantee. The paper also proves an approximate identifiability result, showing that if alignment and Gaussian matching are only approximately optimized, the deviation from a linear latent map is bounded. Finally, it connects identifiability to control by proving that linear recovery of the latent state is sufficient for optimal latent-space planning.4d:T4e4,The experiments are designed to validate the theory rather than to maximize benchmark scores. The paper includes several regimes. First, low-dimensional synthetic mixing experiments test whether LeJEPA recovers a representation linearly aligned with the true latent variables under nonlinear observation maps, with non-Gaussian latent distributions used as ablations. Second, the setup is scaled to 1024-dimensional latents to show that the phenomenon is not confined to toy 2D cases. Third, distributional ablations systematically vary the latent distribution and measure how identifiability degrades as one moves away from Gaussianity, matching the converse and approximate-identifiability claims. Fourth, pixel-based robotic control and latent-space planning experiments support the claim that linear identifiability is sufficient for optimal planning in the learned representation space. The provided text fragment does not include the full result tables, so it would be unsafe to invent exact gains. What matters in the experimental section is the structure of the evidence: necessity of the Gaussian assumption, graceful degradation under approximate objective satisfaction, and the link between identifiable representations and planning quality.4e:T510,Read it as a theory paper with empirical checks, not as a benchmark paper. Start with the introduction and the formal problem setup to fix the paper’s notion of a world model: a representation that linearly recovers latent variables, not merely one that predicts future observations. Then read the world assumptions carefully: independent latents, stationarity, additive noise, and why Gaussian latents imply an OU transition. The main technical substance is in the forward theorem and the converse theorem. In the forward direction, focus on how the spectral decomposition over Gaussian space makes every nonlinear degree pay an alignment penalty. In the converse direction, focus on why Gaussianity is unique rather than merely sufficient. If time is limited, prioritize the theorem statements, proof sketches, the approximate-identifiability result, and the proposition connecting identifiability to latent planning, then inspect the synthetic and control experiments. The most useful questions to carry while reading are: is Gaussian regularization acting mainly as a stabilizer or as an inductive bias, and what kind of regularizer would be needed to extend the theory beyond Gaussian worlds? A quick pass takes about 30-45 minutes; a serious read of the proofs takes substantially longer.4f:T72d,The experimental design is clean and well targeted. The authors use a generator–probe setup. A generator model first produces a full chain-of-thought rationale. That rationale is then injected into the context of a separate probe model as an assistant response, and the probe predicts the final answer. This separates rationale generation from rationale consumption. To avoid trivial leakage, explicit answer-declaration sentences are automatically removed before any perturbation is applied.

The core method is a hierarchy of structure-destroying perturbations. Sentence Shuffle preserves each sentence but randomizes sentence order, testing whether sentence-level derivation order matters.
